L68: ## 1 Executive Summary
L69: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L70: Trade name:	Inqovi®
Established name:	decitabine and cedazuridine
Also known as:	ASTX727
Dosage form:	Tablets: 35 mg decitabine and 100 mg cedazuridine
Therapeutic class:	Antineoplastic
Chemical class:	Small molecule
Pharmacologic class:	nucleoside metabolic inhibitor (decitabine),
cytidine deaminase inhibitor (cedazuridine)
Mechanism of action:	hypomethylating agent (decitabine) is antineoplastic agent
L71: The Applicant submitted NDA 212576 for decitabine/cedazuridine tablet, also known as ASTX727, for the treatment of patients with previously treated and untreated myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS) and chronic myelomonocytic leukemia (CMML). Decitabine is a hypomethylating agent approved as the intravenous formulation for the same indication sought by the Applicant. Cedazuridine is a new molecular entity (NME) and is a cytidine deaminase inhibitor that prevents the rapid metabolism of decitabine in the gastrointestinal tract to allow for oral administration. The NDA provides for a fixed dose combination tablets of 35 mg of decitabine and 100 mg of cedazuridine which when given once daily for 5 days provides comparable cumulative exposure to the IV decitabine 5-day regimen of 20 mg/m2 by intravenous infusion over 1 hour daily for 5 days. Decitabine/cedazuridine fixed dose tablet is administered one tablet daily on days 1-5 of a 28-day cycle until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.
L72: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L73: The review teams conclude that the information submitted by the Applicant has provided substantial evidence for the effectiveness of decitabine/cedazuridine for the treatment of adult patients with previously treated and untreated myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS) and chronic myelomonocytic leukemia (CMML) and supports regular approval of this indication. This approval was based on the results of a phase 1/2 study (ASTX727-01) and a randomized phase 3 study (ASTX727-02). The recommendation is based on demonstration of equivalent exposure over 5 days and no erosion of efficacy compared to the 5-day IV decitabine dosing. The geometric mean ratio (90% CI) for the 5-day cumulative decitabine exposure between decitabine/cedazuridine and IV decitabine was 0.99 (0.93, 1.06). In the phase 2 study, the complete response (CR) rate was 18% (95% CI: 10, 28) with duration of response of 8.7 months (range: 1.1, 18.2). In the phase 3 study, the CR rate was 21% (95% CI: 15, 29) with duration of response of 7.5 months (range: 1.7, 17.5).
L74: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L75: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L76: Decitabine and cedazuridine tablets, also known as ASTX727, are a fixed-dose combination drug product that contains the active ingredients decitabine (35 mg) and cedazuridine (100 mg). Decitabine is a hypomethylating agent approved as intravenous formulation for the treatment of myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) and chronic myelomonocytic leukemia (CMML). The oral bioavailability of decitabine is limited by degradation of decitabine by cytidine deaminase (CDA) enzyme activity present in the gastrointestinal tract and liver of humans. Cedazuridine is a CDA inhibitor, and oral administration of cedazuridine with decitabine enhances the oral bioavailability of decitabine. The proposed dose of decitabine and cedazuridine tablets provides systemic decitabine exposure comparable to the approved dose of intravenous decitabine.
L77: MDS is a heterogeneous group of diseases characterized by ineffective, dysplastic hematopoiesis leading to cytopenias. Clinical presentation of MDS is a manifestation of cytopenias including fatigue, infections, and bleeding and risk of transformation to acute myeloid leukemia (AML) which is rapidly fatal without treatment. Patients with MDS have an increased risk of mortality. Current prognostic classification is the International Prognostic Scoring System-Revised (IPSS-R), and higher-risk MDS is classified as IPSS-R intermediate- (score &gt;3.5), high-, or very high-risk MDS. Higher-risk MDS can be treated in the frontline setting with hypomethylating agents, azacitidine or decitabine. CMML is a related hematopoietic disorder characterized by peripheral blood monocytosis. CMML was previously classified as MDS and is currently classified as myelodysplastic/myeloproliferative neoplasm (MDS/MPN), and the treatment approach is similar to that for MDS.
L78: Decitabine was approved as the intravenous formulation in 2006 for treatment of adult patients with previously treated and untreated, de novo and secondary MDS and CMML. In clinical studies leading to the approval of intravenous decitabine, the overall response rate (ORR) was 16-17% with complete response (CR) rate of 9-15%.
L79: Because of extensive clinical experience with intravenous decitabine, the development of ASTX727 was designed such that oral administration of ASTX727 over 5 days would result in comparable exposure to intravenous decitabine administered daily for 5 days. The clinical program would then provide evidence of similar safety and efficacy profile compared to intravenous decitabine. The demonstration of comparable exposure, no erosion of efficacy, and similar safety profile was based on a phase 2 study (ASTX727-01-B) and a phase 3 study (ASTX727-02) in adult patients with MDS and CMML, for which intravenous decitabine is currently approved. Both studies were randomized, open-label, 2-sequence crossover studies where patients received either oral ASTX727 or intravenous decitabine in cycles 1 and 2 with two-way crossover,
L80: and the remaining cycles were oral ASTX727. The primary endpoints for each study were 5-day cumulative exposure. Secondary endpoints included safety and efficacy including overall response rate and rates of transfusion independence. The acceptable response rate for MDS appropriate for regulatory decision-making is ORR defined as CR+PR responses. There were no PR responses on the Phase 2 or Phase 3 trials of ASTX727, and therefore, only CR responses were considered for demonstration of clinical benefit.
L81: The geometric mean ratio for the 5-day cumulative decitabine exposure between decitabine/cedazuridine and IV decitabine was 0.99 (90% CI: 0.93, 1.06) in the phase 3 study. In study ASTX727-01-B in 80 patients, the CR rate was 18% (95% CI: 10, 28) with duration of response of 8.7 months (range: 1.1, 18.2). In study ASTX727-02 in 133 patients, the CR rate was 21% (95% CI: 15, 29) with duration of response of 7.5 months (range: 1.7, 17.5). Notably, the initial results from ASTX727-02 did not provide sufficient time on study resulting in low response rates and relatively short durability; however, a later data cutoff included at the 120-day safety update provided longer follow up. The CR responses were sufficiently durable to demonstrate clinical benefit in this patient population. The durability was slightly lower than with IV decitabine, however, the time on treatment was shorter and many of the responses were ongoing at the time of data cutoff, and thus, the median duration of CR would be expected to increase with longer follow-up. The CR benefit was supported by a transfusion benefit on both trials, which demonstrated that 49-53% of patients who were dependent on transfusions at baseline achieved at least 56 days transfusion independence post-baseline and 63-64% of patients who were independent of transfusions at baseline were able to maintain that transfusion independence for at least 56 days post-baseline.
L82: Safety data was pooled across studies ASTX727-01-B and ASTX727-02 given the similar study designs and patient populations. Comparative review of safety between ASTX727 and IV decitabine was focused in the first cycle of therapy to avoid the possibility of overlapping toxicities between the first and second cycles of therapy. Evaluation of safety across all cycles included one cycle of IV decitabine, but because adverse reactions cannot be easily attributed to one cycle, the IV decitabine cycle was included in safety analysis. Overall, the adverse reactions appear to be more common in patients treated with ASTX727 compared to IV decitabine in the first cycle of therapy. This observation may have been due to imbalances in baseline demographics. It will be important for clinicians to closely monitor patients while on this oral form of decitabine given the possibility for significant cytopenias leading to increased risk of infection and bleeding. Despite this being an oral therapy that will allow patients to spend less time in the clinic, it will be important to have close follow-up for laboratory testing and clinical monitoring. The important risks of myelosuppression and embryo-fetal toxicity are unchanged from IV decitabine based on the data presented in the submission. Overall, given the historically poor outcomes of patients with MDS who are candidates for hypomethylating agent therapy, the level of toxicity observed with ASTX727 is acceptable for the clinical benefit observed.
L83: In conclusion, the submitted clinical information from studies ASTX727-01-B and ASTX727-02 provided data for equivalent 5-day exposure of decitabine when administered as 35 mg decitabine and 100 mg cedazuridine tablets compared to the 5-day regimen of intravenous decitabine. Similar efficacy was demonstrated by CR rate and duration of CR supported by transfusion independence. The safety profile was also similar between oral ASTX727 and intravenous decitabine with small increase in myelosuppression which is manageable with monitoring and dose modification, if needed. While the treatment regimen evaluated included one cycle of IV decitabine for each patient, treatment with decitabine typically provides responses over several cycles of therapy, and the single IV cycle likely did not specifically contribute to efficacy of the oral regimen. Administering all cycles as oral decitabine and cedazuridine tablets is expected to have similar safety and effectiveness as intravenous decitabine. This provides for an oral route of administration of decitabine which may reduce time in clinic, and it will be important for prescribers to monitor compliance and adverse events closely.

L93: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L94: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L95: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L96: Myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) include a heterogeneous group of hematopoietic stem cell disorders characterized by dysplastic changes in myeloid, erythroid, and megakaryocytic progenitors and associated with cytopenias affecting one or more of the three lineages (Steensma 2018). Median age at diagnosis is approximately 70 years, and prognosis is generally poor with median survival times of 1.2 and 0.4 years in patients with intermediate (Int)-2 and high-risk MDS, respectively (Steensma 2018; Greenberg et al 1997). The disease affects more males than females, and among different racial groups, White individuals have the highest incidence (Garcia-Manero 2015; Ma et al 2007). MDS may evolve into acute myeloid leukemia (AML) in one-third of patients (Shukron et al 2012).
L97: Chronic myelomonocytic leukemia (CMML) is characterized by a persistent peripheral blood monocytosis \ge 1 \times 10^9/L with monocytes accounting for \ge 10\% of white blood cells (WBCs) (Solary and Itzykson 2017). The condition is currently categorized by the World Health Organization (WHO) as a myelodysplastic/ myeloproliferative neoplasm (MDS/MPN) (Arber et al 2016). However, as CMML was previously classified as MDS, the treatment approach is similar to that for MDS.
L98: According to the US-based Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) program, a total of 28,138 new cases of MDS were diagnosed between 2012-2016 in the US, corresponding to an age-adjusted incidence rate of 4.5 per 100,000 persons. This estimate excluded a total of 2,961 cases of CMML, which corresponds to a CMML-specific age-adjusted incidence rate of 0.5 per 100,000. Between 2012 and 2016, 90% of cases in the US were diagnosed in patients \ge 60 years of age (Howlader et al 2019).
L99: Patients with MDS are at risk of complications of cytopenia and AML transformation. Functional defects or other comorbidities, such as cardiac or pulmonary conditions, may increase the risk of complications of anemia, iron overload due to transfusion, or bleeding due to thrombocytopenia (Steensma 2018). The main prognostic classification system for MDS is the International Prognostic Scoring System (IPSS), which was introduced in 1997 (Greenberg et al 1997) and revised in 2012 (IPSS-Revised [IPSS-R]) (Greenberg et al 2012). Both systems are widely used. The IPSS-R uses slightly different cytopenia cutoff points and incorporates the MDS cytogenetic score. Using IPSS criteria, expected median survival for Int-1, Int-2, and high risk MDS are 3.5, 1.2, and 0.4 years, respectively (Greenberg et al 1997). Using the comparable risk
L100: groups in the IPSS-R, the expected median survival for intermediate, high risk, and very high risk groups are 3, 1.6, and 0.8 years, respectively (Greenberg et al 2012).
L101: There is no universally adopted risk classification system for CMML, but several classifications recognized the prognostic importance of total WBCs, thus differentiating dysplastic CMML (lower WBCs) from proliferative CMML (higher WBCs). More recently, the 2016 WHO classification defined 3 groups (CMML-0; CMML-1; and CMML-2) based on percentage blasts in peripheral blood and bone marrow (Arber et al 2016). Patients with CMML are generally diagnosed between age 65 and 75 years with a 2:1 male predominance (Parihk and Tefferi 2012). Similar to MDS, the main manifestations are those of the underlying cytopenia, including fatigue and dyspnea due to anemia, susceptibility to infections, and bleeding (although less common). Median survival ranges from approximately 9 to 20 months (Parihk and Tefferi 2012).
L102: In conclusion, both MDS and CMML are serious and life-threatening myeloid malignancies with short median survival and life-threatening complications due to cytopenias and risk of transformation to AML.
L103: ## The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:
L104: FDA agrees that both MDS and CMML are serious diseases with a substantial risk of mortality.
L105: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L106: The only FDA-approved treatments for Intermediate and high-risk MDS and CMML are the hypomethylating agents azacitidine (Vidaza, FDA approved in 2004) and decitabine (Dacogen, FDA approved in 2006) (Table 1). Lenalidomide (Revlimid) is only approved in transfusion-dependent anemia in patients with low or intermediate-1 risk MDS with 5q deletion so it is not of relevance for patients with higher risk MDS or CMML.
L107: Decitabine (Dacogen®) is administered as a 3-day regimen (15 mg/m2 over a 3-hour intravenous [IV] infusion repeated every 8 hours for 3 days) or a 5-day regimen (20 mg/m2 over a 1-hour IV infusion repeated daily for 5 days) (Dacogen USPI 2019). Based on published data, the 5-day regimen has been globally adopted as the standard regimen due to its improved convenience, better tolerability, lower total cycle dose (100 mg/m2 compared with 135 mg/m2), and comparable benefit-risk profile compared with the 3-day regimen. Almost all current clinical studies of IV decitabine use the 5 day regimen. Azacitidine (Vidaza®) is administered daily for 7 days (starting dose of 75 mg/m2 subcutaneous [SC] or IV) (Vidaza USPI 2018). Both drugs have improved the outcomes of intermediate- and high-risk MDS patients with a median survival time of 14 to 24 months (Kantarjian et al 2006; Fenaux et al 2009; Zeidan et al 2018).

L115: Source: USPIs for Dacogen, Vidaza, and Revlimid
L116: ## The Applicant's Position:
L117: Within the previous 15 years, agents approved for treatment of higher-risk MDS and CMML (decitabine, azacitidine) have improved patient outcomes (Kantarjian et al 2006, Fenaux et al 2009) with higher response rates, hematological improvement, and higher transfusion independence than either best supportive care, or other conventional care regimens. Azacitidine also showed improved survival over conventional care regimens (Fenaux et al 2009). This is believed to be related to the prolonged treatment (median of 9 azacitidine cycles) in the AZA-001 study where azacitidine was given as long as the patient continues to benefit (Fenaux et al 2009). Decitabine MDS studies suffered from a design flaw where decitabine was only given for 2 cycles after achieving CR, or discontinued if a patient did not achieve at least partial response (PR) after 6 cycles. This resulted in a much shorter number of cycles in the decitabine study (median 3 cycles) (Kantarjian et al 2006). It is now globally acknowledged that both agents should be given continuously since stopping treatment is associated with quick relapse (Cabrero et al 2015), and that patients with stable disease and no objective response can still have a survival benefit (Silverman et al 2011; Papageorgiou et al 2018). Both Dacogen and Vidaza USPIs recommend treatment for at least 4 to 6 cycles, and that treatment continues as long as the patient benefits.
L118: The importance of treatment continuation for several months and sometimes years as long as the patient is benefitting results in significant burden on MDS patients. Patients with MDS are often over the age of 70 years and frequently have comorbidities or limited mobility that hinder their ability to attend daily hospital/clinic visits for multiple days each month to receive IV infusions or large volume subcutaneous injections, which can extend over several months or years, particularly in responding patients. This burden may ultimately lead to premature treatment discontinuation and put patients at risk of relapse, since HMA treatment must be continued for as long as the patient continues to benefit. At the same time complication of IV and subcutaneous administrations may result in infections and/or bleeding complications that may be serious in the context of severe neutropenia and thrombocytopenia that these patients often have.
L119: An orally available hypomethylating agent (HMA) should substantially reduce the burden of treatment for patients and their caregivers. Identification of an orally bioavailable HMA agent has been challenging, primarily due to the rapid metabolism of nucleosides by cytidine deaminase (CDA) in the gastrointestinal (GI) mucosa and liver. High levels of CDA in the GI tract and liver rapidly degrade nucleosides upon oral administration and limit or prohibit their oral bioavailability. For example, to achieve even modest exposures of azacitidine requires administration of large doses, which are associated with Grade 3 and Grade 4 GI toxicity (e.g., nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea) and high variability in systemic exposures (Garcia-Manero et al 2011). The applicant believes that an oral HMA that provides equivalent systemic exposure to its parenteral form will alleviate the significant burden on MDS patients and their caregivers, and reduce utilization of hospital/clinic resources needed for parenteral administration. It also has the potential of achieving better long term treatment compliance, and fewer adverse
L120: reactions related to multiple parenteral administrations.
L121: ## **The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:**
L122: FDA agrees that current treatment options for MDS include decitabine, azacitidine, and lenalidomide, but that lenalidomide is only approved for patients with transfusion-dependent anemia due to low- or intermediate-1-risk MDS associated with a deletion 5q abnormality with or without additional cytogenetic abnormalities. Both decitabine and azacitidine are approved for patients with MDS, with slight differences in their indication statements, as outlined by the Applicant above. Of note, the azacitidine indication for the FAB subtypes of RA and RARS is limited to patients with neutropenia or thrombocytopenia or requiring transfusions, whereas this is not mentioned in the decitabine indication. Furthermore, the decitabine indication notes the inclusion of patients with de novo and secondary MDS, whereas this is not mentioned in the azacitidine indication statement. For decitabine, intermediate-1, intermediate-2, and high-risk IPSS is stipulated, whereas this is not mentioned in the indication statement for azacitidine. Lastly, the decitabine indication includes previously treated and untreated patients given that a subset of patients on the supportive trials received previous therapy for their MDS.
L123: In practice, despite the small differences in the wording of the drug labels, both drugs are used interchangeably in the frontline setting for the treatment of patients with MDS, predominately in patients with higher-risk MDS (IPSS-R intermediate [score &gt; 3.5], high, or very high or IPSS intermediate-2 or high risk) based on NCCN guidelines (NCCN 2020).
L124: ## 3 Regulatory Background
L125: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L126: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L127: ASTX727 is not currently marketed in the United States. ASTX727 fixed-dose combination (FDC) tablets are composed of 100 mg cedazuridine and 35 mg decitabine. Cedazuridine is a new molecular entity (NME). IV decitabine has been FDA approved for intermediate and high-risk MDS and CMML since 2006, and approved in the EU for previously untreated AML not candidates for intensive chemotherapy since 2012.
L128: ## **The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:**
L129: FDA confirms the Applicant's assessment.

L138: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L139: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L140: The Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI) conducted inspections for Studies ASTX727-01-B and ASTX727-02 at clinical sites in Nashville, TN (Vanderbilt-Ingram University Medical Center) and Houston, TX (MD Anderson Cancer Center). These sites had the highest accrual, greatest impact on the complete remission rate, (b) (4), (b) (6) Inspection review of both clinical sites determined that data derived from both sites were considered reliable. The classification for both sites was No Action Indicated.
L141: The Applicant (Astex Pharmaceuticals, Inc.) was also audited. The classification for the Applicant inspection was also No Action Indicated. Based on these inspection results, the study data derived from the inspected clinical sites were considered reliable in support of the requested indication.
L142: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L143: Decitabine and cedazuridine (Inqovi) is presented as a fixed-dose combination (FDC), biconvex, red, oval-shaped, film-coated tablet for oral use containing 35 mg of decitabine and 100 mg of cedazuridine. The tablets are plain-faced on one side and debossed with "H35" on the other side. Inactive ingredients include lactose monohydrate, hypromellose, croscarmellose sodium, colloidal silicon dioxide, and magnesium stearate. The tablet coating includes polyvinyl alcohol-(b) (4) titanium dioxide, (b) (4) talc, and iron oxide red. All excipients are compendial-compliant. The drug product is supplied in a blister package formed by aluminum foil on foil lidding containing one tablet per cavity with an expiry of (b) (4) months when stored at USP controlled room temperature.
L144: The Applicant indicated that the commercial tablet formulation (i.e., the final FDC) is similar to the early FDC clinical trial tablet formulation used in Study ASTX727-01-B, differing only in (b) (4) addition of the red film coat and debossing. The Phase 3 Study ASTX727-02 used the final FDC tablet formulation. Furthermore, decitabine and cedazuridine administered as separate capsules, used during the Dose Confirmation stage of the Phase 2 Study ASTX727-01-B, was found to have similar exposure to the early FDC and final FDC formulations. Based on the data submitted, the Biopharmaceutics reviewer concluded that the clinical trial and commercial formulations were comparable, so the data from Studies ASTX727-01-B and ASTX727-02 were acceptable for use in the assessment of efficacy and clinical safety.
L145: There were no outstanding safety issues identified for the manufacturing process. Facilities
L146: inspections have been completed. The Applicant claimed a categorical exclusion from the requirement for an environmental assessment, and the claim was accepted under 21 CFR 25.31(b). No product quality concerns were identified on CMC review, and approval is recommended.
L147: ## 4.3. **Clinical Microbiology**
L148: Not applicable.
L149: ## 4.4. **Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues**
L150: Not applicable. No devices or companion diagnostics are proposed for this application.
L151: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L152: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L153: ASTX727 is an oral fixed-dose combination (FDC) drug product that contains the active ingredients decitabine and cedazuridine (E7727). Decitabine (Dacogen®) for injection is an approved nucleoside metabolic inhibitor; the oral bioavailability of decitabine is limited by cytidine deaminase (CDA) activity present in the gastrointestinal (GI) tract and liver of humans. CDA enzymatically deaminates cytidine analogs to generate pharmacologically inactive metabolites. Substrates of CDA include the cytidine analogs decitabine and gemcitabine. Cedazuridine is a CDA inhibitor, and oral administration of cedazuridine with decitabine enhances the oral bioavailability of decitabine. The proposed dose of ASTX727 provides systemic decitabine exposure comparable to the approved dose of decitabine for injection. While cedazuridine is not an anti-tumor drug, the combination of decitabine and cedazuridine as an FDC drug product is indicated to treat patients with cancer; thus, the nonclinical development program of cedazuridine will follow the recommendations in ICH S9 and ICH S9 Questions and Answers. A complete nonclinical program for cedazuridine is not necessary because of the combination and known toxicities associated with decitabine, for which the Applicant has the right of reference.
L154: Cedazuridine inhibited the activity of human CDA in vitro (IC_{50}=0.281 \muM) and improved the in vitro half-life of gemcitabine in the presence of human CDA from t_{1/2} &lt; 36 minutes to t_{1/2} &gt; 66 hours. Cedazuridine demonstrated negligible anti-proliferative activity in vitro at concentrations up to 10 \muM. The in vivo effect of cedazuridine on the antitumor activity of orally administered decitabine was assessed in the L1210 and KG-1 disseminated leukemia mouse models. In both mouse tumor models the dose of cedazuridine was varied (1 to 100 mg/kg, oral) while a fixed dose of decitabine was administered (0.3 or 0.4 mg/kg, oral). Control arms for oral cedazuridine monotherapy, oral decitabine monotherapy, and intravenous (IV) decitabine monotherapy were included. Decitabine was administered 30-40 minutes after cedazuridine to allow time-delay inhibition of CDA; contrariwise, cedazuridine and decitabine are delivered simultaneously in the ASTX727 FDC drug product. In both mouse tumor models oral cedazuridine administered in combination with oral decitabine conferred an increase in life span (31% to 88%, relative to vehicle control), but the effect was dose-dependent only in the L1210 disseminated tumor model. Neither oral cedazuridine nor oral decitabine monotherapy resulted in an increase in life span relative to vehicle control.
L155: There were no cardiovascular or respiratory findings in a single-dose standalone safety pharmacology study in cynomolgus monkeys with oral doses of cedazuridine up to 200 mg/kg. In a 13-week toxicity study, cynomolgus monkeys were orally administered repeated doses of cedazuridine at up to 200 mg/kg/day with no remarkable electrocardiograph (ECG) findings. Neither cedazuridine nor its metabolites cross the blood brain barrier. No safety pharmacology
L156: studies were conducted with ASTX727 or decitabine.
L157: The intended pharmacologic effect of cedazuridine is to improve the oral bioavailability of decitabine; accordingly, the Applicant conducted a series of studies to evaluate the absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) profiles of cedazuridine, decitabine, and ASTX727. Toxicokinetics (TK) were also evaluated as part of the general toxicology studies for cedazuridine (in mice and cynomolgus monkeys) and decitabine (in rats). Orally administered cedazuridine was rapidly absorbed in both species with a plasma T_{max} of ~1 hour in mice and ~2 hours in cynomolgus monkeys. In a time-delay study in cynomolgus monkeys 2-hour pretreatment with oral cedazuridine greatly enhanced exposure to subsequent orally administered decitabine (up to 17-fold increase in AUC_{0-t}, relative to oral decitabine monotherapy); concomitant oral administration of cedazuridine and decitabine resulted in a 6.4-fold increase in decitabine AUC_{0-t}, relative to oral decitabine monotherapy.
L158: The distribution, metabolism, and excretion profiles of cedazuridine were evaluated in mass balance studies in mice and cynomolgus monkeys. In mice, orally administered [^{14}C]-cedazuridine distributed primarily into the metabolic/excretory and GI tissues and did not pass the blood brain barrier. The metabolite profile of [^{14}C]-cedazuridine was similar in mice and cynomolgus monkeys, which primarily consisted of unmodified cedazuridine and cedazuridine epimer, followed by less abundant metabolites resultant of dehydrogenation, oxidation, and urea conjugation. All significant human metabolites of cedazuridine were detected in mice and/or monkeys. In both mice and cynomolgus monkeys, the excretion profile of orally administered cedazuridine was primarily fecal, and some fecal excretion could be attributed to unabsorbed drug. In the general toxicology studies, cedazuridine (mice and cynomolgus monkeys) and decitabine (rats) were administered orally once daily for 5 to 7 days in 28-day cycles, similar to the clinical regimen. There were no sex-related differences in PK parameters or significant accumulation of cedazuridine or decitabine.
L159: A series of studies were conducted to assess the toxicity of cedazuridine or decitabine monotherapy, or cedazuridine in combination with decitabine. A literature assessment submitted by the Applicant concluded that humans, mice, and cynomolgus monkeys have similar CDA tissue distribution and activity profiles, and CDA activity was notably absent in rats. The Applicant concluded mice and cynomolgus monkeys were the appropriate species to evaluate the toxicity of cedazuridine. Rats were chosen to evaluate the toxicity of oral decitabine given the relatively high oral bioavailability in this species. The pivotal studies were conducted under GLP-compliance, and the test articles were administered by the oral route following dosing regimens comparable to the planned clinical treatment cycle of 5 consecutive dosing days followed by 23 non-dosing days. Cedazuridine monotherapy was assessed in repeat-dose toxicity studies up to 13-weeks/4-cycles in duration in mice (up to 1000 mg/kg/dose) and cynomolgus monkeys (up to 200 mg/kg/dose). Findings in mice included bone marrow cytology changes, non-adverse changes in hematology parameters, increase in spleen
L160: weight, and histopathologic changes in the bone marrow and spleen (hematopoiesis), small intestine (hyperplasia), and male (bilateral oligospermia of the epididymis) and female (follicular degeneration of the ovary) reproductive tracts. All findings were reversible following a 3-week recovery period; the testicular findings were partially recovered with a reduction in severity following the recovery period. Findings in cynomolgus monkeys were more limited and included bone marrow cytology changes, changes in hematology parameters (particularly red blood cell parameters), and histopathologic changes in the bone marrow (atypia) and GI tract (mitoses/single cell death and atypia), all of which were reversible following a 3-week recovery period.
L161: Pivotal studies of oral decitabine monotherapy were limited to a 13-week/4-cycle study conducted in rats only (up to 7.5 mg/kg/dose). Observations in rats included changes in hematology parameters, bone marrow cytology changes, decreased thymus, spleen, testis and epididymis weights, and degenerative histopathological findings in the bone marrow, thymus, spleen, intestines, and male reproductive organs. Following the 3-week recovery period findings were reversible; the changes in the male reproductive organs persisted but showed reduced incidence and severity. The findings for orally administered decitabine were consistent with the known toxicological profile of IV administered decitabine.
L162: The toxicologic profiling of cedazuridine in combination with decitabine was limited to a non-GLP 4-week/1-cycle study in cynomolgus monkeys. This study did not evaluate the ASTX727 FDC drug product; instead, monkeys were orally administered separate gelatin capsules containing cedazuridine (1, 3, or 6 mg/kg/day) and decitabine (2 mg/kg/day). This study did not identify any novel findings. TK analyses identified increased systemic exposure to decitabine with increasing doses of cedazuridine. Additional combination studies were not conducted due to the disparate toxic dose levels of decitabine and cedazuridine.
L163: Cedazuridine was evaluated in a standard battery of GLP-compliant genotoxicity studies. Genetic toxicity studies for cedazuridine were not requested by the FDA because of the combination therapy that included a genotoxic drug. Cedazuridine was mutagenic in the Ames assay and genotoxic in the in vitro chromosome aberration assay, but not genotoxic in the in vivo micronucleus assay. The genotoxic potential of cedazuridine and decitabine will be described in the product label for ASTX727. Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted and are not warranted to support a marketing application for the proposed indication.
L164: No stand-alone reproductive and developmental toxicology studies were conducted with cedazuridine or ASTX727. Decitabine is teratogenic, fetotoxic, and embryotoxic based on its mechanism of action and animal studies described in the product label for decitabine for injection; these risks will be described in the product label for ASTX727. In the repeat-dose toxicity study in mice cedazuridine caused adverse effects in male (bilateral oligospermia of the epididymis) and female (follicular degeneration of the ovary) reproductive tracts, but these were observed at exposures &gt;100-fold the human exposures. A mouse fertility study described
L165: in the product label for decitabine for injection and the repeat-dose toxicity study of oral decitabine in rats identified adverse effects in the male reproductive tract. Risks to fertility due to cedazuridine and decitabine will be described in the product label for ASTX727.
L166: Due to the potential for ASTX727 to cause fetal harm, the use of effective contraception is recommended for female or male patients while receiving ASTX727 and for 6 or 3 months following the last dose, respectively. The recommendations for duration of contraception are based on the FDA guidance "Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations".
L167: There are no data on the presence of cedazuridine, decitabine, or their metabolites in human milk, the effects on the breastfed child, or on milk production. Because of the potential for serious adverse events in a breastfed child, the product label for ASTX727 recommends lactating women to avoid breastfeeding during treatment with ASTX727 and for at least 2 weeks after the last dose. The recommendation for the duration to avoid breastfeeding is consistent with the Dacogen label.
L168: The Applicant proposed acceptance criteria of cedazuridine-related specified impurities in excess of the limits described ICH Q3A. These impurities were qualified in the monkey toxicology studies with safety factors of (b) (4) to greater than (b) (4) times the exposure at the proposed dose of ASTX727. The nonclinical pharmacology, ADME/TK, and toxicology data submitted to this NDA, in combination with the nonclinical data submitted to support the approval of decitabine for injection (NDA 21790), are adequate to support the approval of ASTX727 for the proposed indication.

L177: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L178: ## **Primary pharmacology**
L179: ## **The Regulatory Authority's Assessment:**
L180: Cedazuridine inhibited the recombinant human CDA-catalyzed deamination of cytidine in vitro (IC_{50}=0.281 \muM). High levels of cedazuridine (0.39 mM) increased the in vitro half-life of gemcitabine in the presence of human CDA from t_{1/2} &lt; 36 minutes to t_{1/2} &gt; 66 hours; neither the effect of clinically-relevant levels of cedazuridine on the in vitro half-life of gemcitabine nor the effect of cedazuridine on the in vitro half-life of decitabine were assessed. A literature assessment submitted by the Applicant concluded that among common species used in nonclinical testing, mice and cynomolgus monkeys have CDA tissue distribution and activity profiles most similar to humans. CDA activity was notably absent in the rat. The human, mouse, and cynomolgus monkey CDA amino acid sequences are similar, but not identical. In vitro studies evaluating the effect of cedazuridine on mouse or cynomolgus monkey CDA were not submitted.
L181: The effect of cedazuridine on the proliferation of eight human cancer cell lines, including two acute leukemia cell lines, was assessed in vitro; cedazuridine had no activity on the proliferation of tumor cells over the concentration range tested (3 to 10 \muM) in the cell lines tested.
L182: The effect of cedazuridine on the antitumor activity of decitabine was assessed in two
L183: disseminated leukemia mouse models. In the L1210 disseminated tumor model CD2-F1 mice were inoculated with L1210 mouse ALL cells by IV injection. Three days after inoculation mice were treated orally with cedazuridine (1-100 mg/kg QDx5); 30-40 minutes later the same mice were treated orally with decitabine (0.4 mg/kg QDx5). In the KG-1 disseminated tumor model NSG mice were inoculated with KG-1 human AML cells by IV injection. Twenty-six days after inoculation mice were treated orally with cedazuridine (1-100 mg/kg QDx5); 30-40 minutes later the same mice were treated orally with decitabine (0.3 mg/kg QDx5). In the KG-1 model mice received two cycles of therapy separated by a 23-day non-dosing period. There were control arms for oral cedazuridine monotherapy, oral decitabine monotherapy, and IV decitabine monotherapy in both models. Antitumor activities of cedazuridine and/or decitabine were assessed by median survival time and the percentage increase in life span.
L184: In the L1210 disseminated tumor model, oral cedazuridine treatment administered with a fixed oral dose of decitabine (0.4 mg/kg) resulted in a dose-dependent increase in life span (41-88%, p\le 0.0002), relative to vehicle control (see Figure 1). Intravenous decitabine (0.4 mg/kg) monotherapy resulted in a 47% increase in life span (p&lt;0.0001) relative to vehicle control. Neither oral cedazuridine nor oral decitabine monotherapy resulted in a statistically significant increase in life span relative to vehicle control. The increased life span with the cedazuridine (100 mg/kg) and oral decitabine (0.4 mg/kg) combination relative to IV decitabine (0.4 mg/kg) monotherapy was statistically significant (p&lt;0.0291) and may be attributed to hepatic metabolism of IV administered decitabine.
L185: Figure 1: Survival in the L1210 disseminated tumor mouse model
(Figure excerpted from Applicant's submission)
L186: In the KG-1 disseminated tumor model, oral cedazuridine treatment administered with a fixed oral dose of decitabine (0.3 mg/kg) resulted in a non-dose dependent increase in life span (31-62%, p&lt;0.0001), relative to vehicle control (see Figure 2). Oral and IV decitabine (0.3 mg/kg) monotherapy resulted in a 9% (p=0.0142) and 31% (p&lt;0.0001) increase in life span,
L187: respectively, relative to vehicle control. Oral cedazuridine monotherapy did not result in a statistically significant increase in life span relative to vehicle control. The increased life span with the cedazuridine (10-100 mg/kg) and oral decitabine (0.3 mg/kg) combination relative to IV decitabine (0.3 mg/kg) was statistically significant (p\le 0.0165) and may be attributed to hepatic metabolism of IV administered decitabine.
L188: Figure 2: Survival in the KG-1 disseminated tumor mouse model
(Figure excerpted from Applicant's submission)

L193: No secondary PD studies have been conducted for cedazuridine, the new chemical entity component of ASTX727.
L194: ## The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:
L195: Cedazuridine (1 and 10 \muM) was screened against a panel of 80 human cell receptors, ion channels, and transporters in the presence of reference compounds. Assay conditions varied depending on the target. Neither concentration of cedazuridine had a significant effect on reference compound binding under the conditions tested.
L196: ## Safety Pharmacology
L197: ## Data:
L198: ## A. Cardiovascular
L199: Safety pharmacology studies were conducted with cedazuridine to determine its in vitro effects on the human ether à-go-go related gene (hERG) potassium channel current in stably transfected human embryonic kidney (HEK293) cells (GLP Study/Report No. 100422.FJT) and on action potential parameters in isolated guinea pig papillary muscles (Report No. ER-849727-00). The cumulative data from these studies indicate that cedazuridine does not have any effect on the cardiovascular system as evidenced by an in vitro concentration giving half-maximal inhibition (IC_{50}) value of &gt;300 \mu M for inhibition of hERG channel current and no effects on any action potential parameters in isolated papillary muscles from guinea pigs.
L200: The potential in vivo effects of cedazuridine on the cardiovascular system and on respiratory function were evaluated in a stand-alone GLP-compliant study in conscious, naïve male cynomolgus monkeys using telemetry (Study/Report No. 20123940). Cedazuridine was orally administered as a single dose to male cynomolgus monkeys (n=4) at dose levels of 0.20 and 200 mg/kg/day on Days 1, 3 and 10, respectively. Cedazuridine was well tolerated and had no effects on any cardiovascular parameters up to a single oral dose of 200 mg/kg [the no-observed-effect level (NOEL) in this study].
L201: In addition, cardiovascular assessments included in a 7-day GLP toxicity study in rhesus monkeys (Study/Report No. LFA00080) and a 4-cycle GLP toxicity study in cynomolgus monkeys (Study/Report No. 20124863) showed no abnormalities in the electrocardiogram (ECG) data following repeat once-daily oral administrations of cedazuridine at doses of up to 200 mg/kg/day (the highest dose administered) for 7 consecutive days.
L202: ## B. Respiratory
L203: In a combination cardiovascular function and respiratory assessment study (details discussed above under "Cardiovascular"), a single oral dose of cedazuridine of up to 200 mg/kg was not associated with any effects on respiratory rate, tidal volume, or minute volume in conscious telemetered monkeys (Study/Report No. 20123940).
L204: ## C. Central Nervous System (CNS)
L205: Stand-alone nonclinical safety pharmacology assessment of ASTX727 (or cedazuridine or decitabine) on central nervous system (CNS) function has not been conducted. Cedazuridine and cedazuridine-derived entities do not show a strong tendency to distribute into tissues and, following oral administration, are primarily distributed into the GI tract and metabolic/excretory tissues (e.g., kidney or urinary bladder). Cedazuridine-derived entities do not cross the blood brain barrier (Study No. 17718; Report No. RPT04536).
L206: ## The Applicant's Position:
L207: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## NDA 212576, Original
## INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
L208: The results of safety pharmacology studies did not reveal adverse findings of concern regarding effects of cedazuridine on the cardiovascular system (based on in vitro hERG and actional potential assay data and on in vivo evaluations in monkeys) or the respiratory system (based on evaluations in conscious monkeys). Cedazuridine and cedazuridine-derived entities do not show a strong tendency to distribute into tissues and, following oral administration, are primarily distributed into the gastrointestinal tract and metabolic/excretory tissues (e.g., kidney or urinary bladder). CNS safety pharmacology studies were therefore not conducted. Thus, based on the results from the studies conducted with cedazuridine, no potential safety pharmacology concerns for ASTX727 attributable to cedazuridine have been identified.
L209: ## **The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:**
L210: We agree with the Applicant's conclusions for the safety pharmacology studies conducted with cedazuridine. We note that no safety pharmacology studies were conducted with ASTX727 or decitabine.
L211: ## 5.4. ADME/PK
L212: ## Data:
L213: ## Absorption
L214: ## Comparison of Pharmacokinetic Profiles of Decitabine and E7727 after Single Oral Administration of Two FDC Tablet Forms of ASTX727 using a Crossover Design (Study/Report No. APL-FFS-PK-20170522-01)
L215: In this cross-over study, male cynomolgus monkeys were administered ASTX727 [100 mg cedazuridine (E7727) and 35 mg decitabine] as a fixed-dose combination (FDC) Tablet-A (earlier formulation used in the FDC stage of the Phase 2 clinical trial) or Tablet-B (new formulation, developed for the Phase 3 clinical trial). After a 6-day washout period, the ASTX727 formulation was reversed to complete the crossover. The results of this study indicate that both forms of FDC tablets showed similar PK profiles and exposures for decitabine and cedazuridine. Exposures for both decitabine and cedazuridine were higher on the second day of dosing compared to the first day on each week (i.e., Day 2 vs. Day 1 and Day 9 vs. Day 8). This effect was more pronounced for decitabine than for cedazuridine.

L219: ## Distribution
L220: ## Mass Balance, Radio-Pharmacokinetics in Male and Female CD-1 Mice and Tissue Distribution by Quantitative Whole Body Autoradiography in Female CD-1 and C57BL/6 Mice Following a Single Oral Administration of [^{14}C]E7727
## (Study No. 17718; Report No. RPT04536)
L221: In a quantitative whole-body autoradiography (QWBA) and mass balance study in female C57BL/6 and CD-1 mice, a single oral dose of 300 mg/kg (100 \mu Ci/kg) [^{14}C]-cedazuridine (E7727) was administered and the tissue distribution was profiled for up to 504 hours post-dose. Overall, cedazuridine did not show a strong tendency to distribute into tissues. The metabolic/excretory tissues (kidney, urinary bladder) as well as the tissues of the GI tract contained the highest distribution of [^{14}C]-cedazuridine derived radioactivity. The tissues with lowest relative level of distributions in both strains of mice were brain, spinal cord, eye lens, and white fat, which were all below the quantifiable limit at all sampled time points. Based on a lack of distribution to the brain and spinal cord, it can be assumed that cedazuridine-related drug materials do not pass through the blood brain barrier. In addition, no significant distribution was noted in melanin-containing tissues such as pigmented skin and uveal tract, suggesting that [^{14}C]-cedazuridine derived radioactivity was not preferentially associating with melanin.

L224: ## Metabolism
L225: Based on the metabolite profile of cedazuridine in mice, monkeys, and humans, unchanged cedazuridine is the most abundant circulating entity followed by its epimer which is the most prominent circulating metabolite. Toxicological coverage for metabolites is available as all metabolites detected in vivo in humans were also present in mouse and monkey plasma.

L233: ## Excretion
L234: ## Mass Balance, Radio-Pharmacokinetics in Male and Female CD-1 Mice and Tissue Distribution by Quantitative Whole Body Autoradiography in Female CD-1 and C57BL/6 Mice Following a Single Oral Administration of [^{14}C]E7727
## (Study No. 17718; Report No. RPT04536)
L235: The excretion profile of cedazuridine in mice and monkeys indicates that the majority of the administered oral dose is not absorbed and is excreted in the feces based on approximately 70% to 77% of the [^{14}C]-cedazuridine dose recovered in feces over a 168-hour post-dose period in mass balance studies.

L289: ## TK data from reproductive toxicology studies

L291: ## TK data from Carcinogenicity studies
L292: None
L293: ## The Applicant's Position:
L294: The PK/TK profile of ASTX727 components cedazuridine and decitabine has been well characterized in various in vitro and in vivo nonclinical studies. There were no unexpected findings and the data from these studies are consistent with the pharmacological action of cedazuridine acting as an inhibitor of CDA as shown by significantly increased systemic exposures to decitabine following concomitant oral dosing of cedazuridine and decitabine at clinically relevant doses. Thus, the nonclinical PK/TK data support the clinical administration of ASTX727 on a schedule that involves daily administration for 5 days per dosing cycle (i.e., Days 1 to 5 of each 28-day cycle).
L295: ## The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:
L296: The Applicant stated the nonclinical PK/TK data supported the ASTX727 clinical dosing schedule. The nonclinical PK/TK data did not directly contribute to the clinical efficacy or AUC equivalence data that supported the approval of NDA 212576. We have additional comments on the studies reviewed above and have reviewed additional ADME/PK data.
L297: ## Absorption
L298: In the comparative PK study evaluating FDC tablets of decitabine and cedazuridine in cynomolgus monkeys the t_{1/2} of cedazuridine was 4-5 hours. The higher exposures (AUC_{0-t} and C_{max}) of decitabine following the second day of dosing is consistent with the expected pharmacology of the FDC tablets of decitabine and cedazuridine.
L299: The effect of oral cedazuridine followed by time-staggered oral decitabine on the PK profile of decitabine was evaluated in cynomolgus monkeys. Monkeys were administered a fixed oral dose of 1 mg/kg cedazuridine concomitantly with a fixed oral dose of 3 mg/kg decitabine, or following a 30-minute to 2-hour time-delay. Coadministration with cedazuridine resulted in higher plasma exposures (6.4-fold increase in AUC_{0-t}, 3.9-fold increase in C_{max}) of decitabine, relative to oral decitabine monotherapy. The plasma decitabine exposure increased further
L300: with time delay; when administered 2 hours after cedazuridine, plasma decitabine AUC_{0-t} and C_{max} increased 17.0-fold and 13.2-fold relative to oral decitabine monotherapy, respectively.
L301: ## Metabolism
L302: The metabolite profile of cedazuridine in cynomolgus monkeys was evaluated as part of a mass balance and PK study. In this study, male and female cynomolgus monkeys were administered an oral dose of 20 mg/kg of [^{14}C] cedazuridine; following a 2-week washout period the same monkeys were administered an IV dose of 2 mg/kg of [^{14}C] cedazuridine. The overall metabolite profiles were similar for males and females following oral or IV administration. All significant human metabolites of cedazuridine were detected in mice and/or monkeys.
L303: ## Excretion
L304: The excretion profile of cedazuridine in cynomolgus monkeys was evaluated as part of the mass balance and PK study described above. The excretion profile of orally administered cedazuridine in monkeys was similar to that observed in mice, with the majority of the administered dose excreted in feces. The excretion profile of IV administered cedazuridine in monkeys was primarily urinary. It is unclear if the high fecal excretion of cedazuridine after oral administration could be attributed to unabsorbed drug.
L305: ## TK data from general toxicology studies
L306: There were no sex-related differences in TK parameters for cedazuridine or decitabine. There was also little to no accumulation of cedazuridine after once daily administration, which was the same as for the clinical regimen.
L307: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L308: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L309: ## Data:
L310: Key toxicology findings from the following pivotal studies are summarized below:
L311: - Cedazuridine (E7727): a 4-cycle GLP toxicology study in mice and 4-cycle GLP toxicology study in monkeys
- Decitabine: 4-cycle GLP toxicology study in rats
- Cedazuridine with decitabine (ASTX727): a 1-cycle (5 days of dosing) PK/PD study in monkeys
L312: ## **Study title/ Study number: A Thirteen-Week/Four-Cycle Oral GLP Toxicity Study of E7727 in CD-1 Mice (Study/Report No. 0862-16193).**
L313: ## **Key Study Findings**
L314: - Bone marrow cytology findings: dose levels of 1000 mg/kg/dose were associated with “mildly higher” myeloid:erythroid (M:E) ratios in females and “minimally lower” M:E ratios in males; reversibility in these effects was noted in both sexes following a 3-week recovery period;
- Hematopoiesis in spleen and sternal bone marrow that appeared to be consistent with a regenerative response to a reduction in circulating leukocyte counts; reversibility in these effects was noted following a 3-week recovery period; and
- Adverse histopathology findings in the male and female reproductive tracts that showed partial recovery in males and full reversibility in females at the end of a 3-week recovery period. It is worth noting that full recovery of all spermatogenic stages is expected to take longer than the 35-day period of a full spermatogenic cycle and, therefore, the partial recovery noted in males suggests progression to recovery of these findings.
L315: (b) (4)
L316: Conducting laboratory and location
L317: GLP compliance: Yes
L318: ## **Methods**
L319: Dose and frequency of dosing:	Cedazuridine (E7727): 0 (control), 100, 300, or 1000 mg/kg/day
4 dosing cycles, with each complete cycle consisting of 7 days of dosing followed by a 21-day non-dosing period for a total of 28 days per cycle.
A recovery period of 3 weeks was included at the end of the 4th dosing cycle.
Route of administration:	Oral (gavage)
Formulation/Vehicle:	0.5% methylcellulose
Species/Strain:	Mouse/Hsd:ICR (CD-1®)
Number/Sex/Group:	Main study: 22 animals/sex/group;
Recovery phase: 6 animals/sex/group
Age:	6-8 weeks (prior to first dose)
Satellite groups/ unique design:	An additional 9 animals/sex/group were used for hematology evaluations on Days 8 and 28 (these evaluations were considered to be exploratory in nature and the mean data were not presented in the study report) and an additional 39 animals/sex/group (and
L320: 9/sex/group in the vehicle control group) were used for TK evaluations.
L321: Deviation from study protocol
affecting interpretation of results: No
L322: ## Observations and Results: changes from control
L323: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	No test article-related deaths.
Oral gavage error-related deaths: 1 control male and 3 control females, 2 LD males and 2 LD females, and 1 MD male
Clinical Signs	No test article-related adverse effects
Body Weights	No test article-related adverse effects
Ophthalmoscopy	No test article-related adverse effects
Hematology	No test article-related adverse effects
Clinical Chemistry	No test article-related adverse effects
Gross Pathology	No test article-related adverse effects
Organ Weights	HD: Slight ↑ in the absolute and relative (to body and brain) spleen weights in females that correlated with more severe cases of ↑ splenic myelopoiesis; findings reversible following the 3-week recovery period.
Histopathology	
At all test article dose levels:
Spleen and sternal bone marrow: Hematopoiesis in both organs that appeared to be consistent with a regenerative response to a reduction in circulating leukocyte counts. The relative lack of red cell precursors in the bone marrow was reflected in the reductions in circulating red cells and reticulocytes. Evidence of ongoing loss of all lineages of hematopoietic marrow tissue (specifically, focally ↑ adipocytes and/or macrophage aggregates associated with cell debris) was noted in a few HD females. A ↓ in lymphocyte counts was noted in the mesenteric lymph node (possibly reflecting oral administration) and in the splenic white pulp in MD and HD females and in 2 HD males. At the end of the recovery period, the spleen and bone marrow hematopoietic changes were fully reversible.
Male reproductive tract: Bilateral oligospermia within the epididymis of HD animals, accompanied by the presence of cell debris within the epididymal lumen and degenerative changes (necrosis of single or small groups of germ cells, epithelial vacuolation, and/or germ cell depletion) in the testicular seminiferous epithelium. There was a reduction in severity of the testicular findings at the end of the recovery period, suggesting partial recovery.
Female reproductive tract: Follicular degeneration, characterized by pale, enlarged, and degenerating granulosa cells in HD animals; full reversibility noted in females at the end of the recovery period.

Other evaluations	
Bone marrow cytology findings: "mildly higher" M:E ratio in HD females and a "minimally lower" M:E ratio in HD males. Mild to marked ↓ in lymphocyte percentages and mild ↓ in mean peripheral lymphocyte counts also were noted in both sexes. Reversibility of these bone marrow cytology findings in both sexes at the end of the recovery period.
L324: HD = high dose; LD = low dose; M:E =myeloid:erythroid; MD = mid dose.
L325: ## **Study title/ Study number: A 92 Day Toxicity Study of E7727 Administered via Oral Gavage in Cynomolgus Monkeys Followed by a 21 Day Recovery (Study/Report No. 20124863).**
L326: ## **Key Study Findings**
L327: - Hematology findings consisting of minimally decreased RBC mass [hemoglobin, RBC count, and hematocrit(HCT)] with associated increases in reticulocytes, and a concomitant minimal to mild increase in red cell distribution width; recovery noted for RBC mass (both sexes), reticulocytes (both sexes), and red cell distribution width (females only) at the end of the 21-day recovery period;
- Mild morphologic changes in the erythroid lineage at the low- and mid-dose levels of 20 and 60 mg/kg/day and marked morphologic changes in the myeloid and erythroid lineages at the high dose level of 200 mg/kg/day; all bone marrow cytology changes were reversible following the 21-day recovery period; and
- Histopathology findings in the bone marrow and GI tract in high-dose animals (200 mg/kg/day); all changes were reversible following the 21-day recovery period.
L328: Conducting laboratory and location:
L329: (b) (4)
L330: GLP compliance: Yes
L331: ## **Methods**

4 dosing cycles, with each complete cycle consisting of 7 days of dosing followed by a 21-day non-dosing period for a total of 28 days per cycle.
A recovery period of 21 days was included at the end of the 92-day study period (i.e., following administration of the last dose in the 4th cycle).

L333: ## Observations and Results: changes from control
L334: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	No deaths
Clinical Signs	No test article-related adverse effects
Body Weights	No test article-related adverse effects
Ophthalmoscopy	No test article-related adverse effects
ECG	No test article-related adverse effects
Hematology	HD: minimally ↓ RBC mass (hemoglobin, RBC count, and HCT) on Days 15 and 71 (for females) and on Day 71 (for males), with associated ↑ in reticulocytes at these time points. By Day 91, reticulocytes were moderately to markedly ↓ for both sexes. A concomitant minimal to mild ↑ in red cell distribution width was noted at all time points between Days 15 and 91. Recovery noted for RBC mass (both sexes), reticulocytes (both sexes), and red cell distribution width (females only) on Day 111 (i.e., the end of the 21-day recovery period)
Clinical Chemistry	No test article-related adverse effects
Urinalysis	No test article-related adverse effects
Gross Pathology	No test article-related adverse effects
Organ Weights	No test article-related adverse effects
Histopathology	
Bone marrow: HD: Minimal to marked atypia of the hematopoietic cells (correlating with morphologic changes in bone marrow cytology and characterized by atypia in the erythroid lineage and, at higher severities, atypia in the myeloid lineage) and mild to moderate ↓ in bone marrow cellularity (characterized by ↓ overall cellularity of the erythroid lineage and, to a lesser extent, the myeloid lineage);
GI tract: HD: Minimal to mild ↑ in mitoses/single cell death of the mucosal epithelial cells of the esophagus and tongue and minimal to marked atypia of the mucosal epithelial cells in the stomach, duodenum, jejunum, ileum, cecum, colon, and rectum (changes characterized by variable severity of one or more of the following: ↑ single cell death, ↑ mitotic figures, atypical mitotic figures, loss of cellular polarity, disorganization, crowding and piling of cells, variation in nuclear size, multiple prominent nucleoli, karyomegaly, cytomegaly, villous blunting and fusion, and crypt collapse).
In 1 HD male, changes were noted in the adrenal gland (minimal cortical neutrophilic infiltrates), prostate gland (mild atypia of the glandular epithelium), lymph node (mild ↑ in cellularity), optic nerve (minimal, perivascular mixed cell infiltrates), spleen (mild ↑ in cellularity), and salivary gland (mild zymogen gland depletion) and, in another HD male, changes were noted in the kidneys (aggregates of mononuclear cells and myeloid hematopoietic precursors with occasional mitotic figures within the renal interstitium).
All histopathology changes reversible following the 21-day recovery period.
L335: Other evaluations	
Bone marrow cytology findings: Mild morphologic changes in the erythroid lineage for individual animals in LD and MD groups and generally marked morphologic changes in the myeloid and erythroid lineages in the HD group that indicated abnormal cell maturation and were associated with correlating histopathology. Mildly \uparrow numbers of metarubricytes with lobulated or mildly lobulated nuclei for individual animals in the LD and MD groups, rarely accompanied by \uparrow cell size. Bone marrow changes in the HD group consisted of the following:
In the myeloid lineage: Variable proportions of giant band neutrophils, segmented neutrophils, eosinophils, and basophils (in addition to earlier stage myeloid progenitors for some animals), frequently with abnormal nuclear morphology; and
In the erythroid lineage: Generally, marked \uparrow in the numbers of late stage erythroid progenitors (rubricytes and metarubricytes) with abnormal nuclear morphology consisting of variably sized (often large) lobulated, irregularly shaped, and/or fragmented nuclei. Erythroid lineage cells frequently exhibited asynchronous maturation, generally reflected by \uparrow cytoplasmic volume and hemoglobinization relative to the maturational stage of the nucleus. These changes correlated with histopathology findings in the bone marrow of minimal to marked atypia in one or more hematopoietic lineages.
All bone marrow cytology changes reversible following the 21-day recovery period.
L336: HCT = hematocrit; HD = high dose; LD = low dose; MD = mid dose; RBC = red blood cell
L337: ## **Study title/ Study number: Decitabine: A 90-Day Multicycle Oral Repeated Dose Toxicity Study in Rats with a 59-Day Recovery Period (GLP) (Study/Report No. T73417002-GN)**
L338: ## **Key Study Findings**
L339: - Hematology findings consisting of decreases in RBC and WBC parameters at all treatment dose levels and decreases in platelet counts only at the 7.5 mg/kg/day (high dose) level; changes reversible at the end of a 59-day recovery period;
- Bone marrow changes consisting of mild to moderate decreases in the M:E ratio in low-dose animals and minimal to marked increases in the M:E ratio in mid- and high-dose animals; changes reversible at the end of a 59-day recovery period; and
- Histopathology effects consisting of degenerative changes in the bone marrow, thymus, spleen, intestines, and male reproductive organs.

Platelets: Statistically significant ↓ in platelet counts in HD animals.

L347: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## NDA 212576, Original
## INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
L348: Gross Pathology	↓ thymic size at all dose levels, ↓ testicular size in MD and HD males, and ↓ epididymal size in HD males; the thymic change was almost fully reversible at the end of the recovery period while the testicular and epididymal changes were still present.
Organ Weights	↓ organ weights: thymus (absolute and relative; statistically significant at all dose levels), spleen (absolute and relative; at all dose levels in males), testis (absolute and relative; statistically significant at all dose levels), and epididymis (absolute and relative; statistically significant at HD level). Reversibility noted in the spleen weights (all groups) and thymus weights (LD males and all females) at the end of the recovery period; effects on thymus weights in MD and HD males and the effects on testicular and epididymal weights persisted.
Histopathology	Terminal necropsy: ↓ bone marrow cellularity at all dose levels; thymic lymphoid depletion at all dose levels; splenic lymphoid depletion in HD males and at all dose levels in females; intestinal single cell necrosis/ apoptosis in MD and HD males and at all dose levels in females; testicular degeneration/atrophy, Sertoli cell vacuolation, and Leydig cell hyperplasia in MD and HD males; epididymal oligospermia at all dose levels, epididymal cell debris in MD and HD males, and epididymal single cell necrosis/apoptosis in HD males.
Recovery necropsy: Full/approaching full reversibility in effects in spleen, intestine, thymus and bone marrow; changes in the male reproductive organs were not recovered except for the epididymal single cell necrosis/apoptosis. In addition, testicular degeneration/atrophy, Sertoli cell vacuolation, and epididymal oligospermia appeared at the LD level.
Other evaluations	Bone marrow cytology findings: Mild to moderate ↓ in the M:E ratio in LD animals and minimal to marked ↑ in the M:E ratio in MD and HD animals (consistent with the hematology changes); effects reversible at the end of the recovery period.
L349: HCT = hematocrit; HD = high dose; LD = low dose; M:E =myeloid:erythroid; MD = mid dose; RBC = red blood cell; WBC = white blood cell

- Bone marrow cytology findings of higher M:E ratios among the different groups of treated males (based on higher mean total myeloid cell counts and lower mean total erythroid cell counts); reversible following a 23-day non-dosing period.

## NDA 212576, Original

L363: ## The Applicant's Position:
L364: In summary, the main repeat-dose toxicity findings from studies conducted in mice, rats, and monkeys were characterized by reversible myelosuppression (leukopenia, neutropenia, lymphopenia, and/or decreased platelet counts) due to decitabine, or both (ASTX727), reversible reproductive tract effects due to cedazuridine, and thymic lymphoid depletion (cedazuridine, reversible) and testicular effects (partially reversible) due to decitabine.
L365: ## The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:
L366: We agree with the Applicant's overall conclusions for the 13-week studies of cedazuridine in mice and cynomolgus monkeys. There were similar cedazuridine-related hematology findings observed in both mice and monkeys, although in mice the magnitude of the findings was not adverse and not included in the Applicant's review. In both species, the hematology findings correlated with histopathologic findings in the spleen and/or bone marrow. The Applicant's review also captured histopathology findings in the GI tract of monkeys but did not describe similar findings in mice. Findings in the GI tract of monkeys were generally described as atypia (including increased single cell death and increased mitoses) while findings in mice were described as hyperplasia and not adverse. Hyperplasia in mice may have been indicative of a regenerative response subsequent to necrosis, and thus the etiology of the findings in mice and monkeys may be similar. The lack of ECG findings in the 13-week monkey study were consistent with the absence of findings in the stand-alone safety pharmacology study in cynomolgus monkeys.
L367: The Applicant evaluated the toxicology of orally administered decitabine in the rat. The oral bioavailability of decitabine is relatively high in rats, which is likely attributed to low CDA activity in this species. We agree the rat is a relevant species to evaluate the toxicology of orally administered decitabine. We agree with the Applicant's overall conclusions for the 13-week study of oral decitabine in rats; the results were similar to those after IV administered decitabine, which was reviewed to support the approval of decitabine for injection (NDA 21790).
L368: The toxicologic profiling of the cedazuridine and decitabine combination was limited to a 1-cycle study in cynomolgus monkeys. Additional combination studies were not conducted or deemed necessary.
L369: ## General toxicology; additional studies

L371: No additional general toxicology studies have been conducted.
L372: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L373: Not applicable.
L374: ## **The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:**
L375: We agree that no additional general toxicity studies were conducted or are needed to support the approval of NDA 212576.
L376: ## 5.5.2. Genetic Toxicology
L377: ## **In Vitro Reverse Mutation Assay in Bacterial Cells (Ames)**
L378: ## **Data:**
L379: ## **Study title/ number: E7727: Reverse Mutation Assay in Bacteria (Study/Report No. 963133)**
L380: ## **Key Study Findings:**
L381: - Cedazuridine (E7727) was mutagenic in *Salmonella typhimurium* strain TA1535 both in the presence and absence of external metabolic activation, but only at the highest tested concentration of 5000 µg/plate, and was non-mutagenic in *Salmonella typhimurium* strains TA98, TA100, and TA1537 and in *E. coli* strain WP2 *uvrA* (pKM101) both in the presence and absence of external metabolic activation.
L382: Metabolic Activation	Test Article	Conc (µg/plate)	Revertant Colony Counts (Mean)
TA98	TA100	TA1535	TA1537	WP2 uvrA
With Activation	Cedazuridine	0 (vehicle control)	53	152	28	20	81

L383: Conc = concentration

L385: GLP compliance: Yes
L386: Test system: *Salmonella typhimurium* strains TA98, TA100, TA1535, and TA1537 and *E. coli* strain WP2 *uvrA* (pKM101) were incubated with cedazuridine both in the presence and absence of S9 at the following concentrations: 0 (control), 313, 625, 1250, 2500, or 5000 µg/plate (initial test) and 0 (control), 1250, 1768, 2500, 3536, or 5000 µg/plate (confirmatory test).
L387: ## The Applicant's Position:
L388: It is worth noting that the standard OECD-compliant Ames assay recommends the use of *S. typhimurium* strains TA100 and TA1535 (OECD 1997a). While these strains both contain the same base pair mutation (adenine-thymine to guanine-cytosine), they differ only in that TA100 contains a pKM101 plasmid which is reported to make it more sensitive than TA1535 in detecting bacterial mutagens (Prival and Zeiger 1998). According to Prival and Zeiger (1998), analysis of the data on the 36 chemical substances that were judged to be mutagenic in TA1535 and non-mutagenic or equivocal in TA100 indicates that the absolute increases in revertant colony counts in TA1535 were so small that they could have been missed if the same increases had occurred in a strain with a higher spontaneous background revertant colony count (i.e., TA100). The findings from the Ames assay with cedazuridine reflect the analysis from Prival and Zeiger (1998) in that the increases in the revertant colony counts in TA1535 noted in both the initial and confirmatory tests were only just doubled at 5,000 µg/plate compared to colony counts in TA1535 controls and that such increases were absent in the more sensitive TA100 (Intertek 2018). Thus, the positive findings that were noted only at the highest tested concentration in strain TA1535 are difficult to interpret given the absence of response in the more sensitive strain TA100.
L389: ## The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:
L390: Cedazuridine (5000 µg/plate) was mutagenic in *S. typhimurium* strain TA1535 in the presence of external metabolic activation (2.3-fold increase in mean revertants). In the absence of external metabolic activation (data not shown) cedazuridine (5000 µg/plate) was mutagenic in strain TA1535 in the initial test (2.2-fold increase in mean revertants), but in the confirmatory test the 1.5-fold increase in mean revertants did not meet the prespecified evaluation criteria (≥2 times the concurrent vehicle control).

L400: Test system: Primary cultures of human peripheral blood lymphocytes were treated with cedazuridine for 4 hours in the presence of S9 [+S9; 0 (control), 150, 300, or 600 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL}], absence of S9 [-S9; 0 (control), 75, 150, or 300 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL}], and for 21 hours in the absence of S9 [-S9; 0 (control), 18.8, 37.5, or 75 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL}].
L401: ## The Applicant's Position:
L402: The positive genotoxicity findings in the human lymphocytes were generally only observed in the presence of significant cell toxicity (as indicated by a decrease in the relative mitotic index compared to concurrent controls) at the highest tested concentrations. All other test article concentrations in all test conditions caused lesser degrees of cytotoxicity (as indicated by relative mitotic indices of \ge 77\%) and, with one exception, did not cause increases in the percentage of aberrant cells. Given that the positive genotoxicity results in this study were noted mainly at test article concentrations that caused cytotoxicity levels in excess of the current OECD-recommended maximum of 55 \pm 5\% of the concurrent negative controls (OECD 2016), according to the OECD guideline, care should be taken in interpreting the positive results.
L403: ## The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:
L404: Cedazuridine was genotoxic in the in vitro chromosome aberration assay under the conditions tested. There was a concentration-dependent increase in percent aberrant cells and in the 4-hour treatment with activation, the statistical increase in the aberrant cells occurred at a cedazuridine concentration that did not cause significant cell toxicity (&lt;55\% of concurrent vehicle control).

L423: ## 5.5.3. **Carcinogenicity**
L424: ## **Data:**
L425: No carcinogenicity studies have been conducted with ASTX727 or with its active components, cedazuridine and decitabine.
L426: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L427: Based on its positive genotoxicity, decitabine may be expected to have carcinogenic potential. As such, there is a carcinogenic risk associated with exposure to ASTX727.
L428: ## **The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment**
L429: Per ICH S9, we agree that carcinogenicity studies are not needed to support the approval of ASTX727 for the proposed indication.
L430: ## 5.5.4. **Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology**
L431: ## **Data:**
L432: No stand-alone reproductive and developmental toxicity studies have been conducted with ASTX727 or with its active components, cedazuridine and decitabine.
L433: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L434: Based on its mechanism of action, decitabine is expected to result in adverse reproductive effects. The US prescribing information for decitabine (Dacogen USPI 2019) includes information on the effect of decitabine on postnatal development and reproductive capacity. Histopathology findings in the male and female mouse reproductive tract following administration of cedazuridine were previously described above (in the General Toxicology section) and support potential adverse reproductive effects. Thus, there is a risk of reproductive and developmental toxicity associated with exposure to ASTX727.
L435: ## **The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:**
L436: Cedazuridine and decitabine are genotoxic, and decitabine targets rapidly dividing cells in general toxicology studies; per ICH S9, reproductive and developmental toxicity studies are not needed for this class of drugs. These drugs are expected to cause malformation or embryofetal lethality.
